Abstract: With the emergence of specification-driven development (SDD), software modeling has regained strategic importance as a way to translate human intentions, requirements, and design decisions into precise specifications that can guide both developers and AI-assisted development tools. Despite the renewed relevance of modeling in SDD, empirical evidence remains limited on when its benefits outweigh the socio-technical effort required to create, communicate, synchronize, and maintain software models in industrial practice. This study investigates when software modeling generates value in industrial practice. We analyzed 405 survey responses collected from software practitioners. The questionnaire combined closed items concerning UML use, benefits, barriers, and improvement mechanisms with open-ended questions about current use, difficulties, and worthwhile contexts. Most respondents associated modeling with integration discussions (90 percent) and shared understanding (82 percent), but also reported time pressure (85 percent) and the effort required to create and maintain models (80 percent). Selective modeling, model-code synchronization, and collaborative support emerged as conditions that improve its viability, whereas only 24 percent reported daily UML use. Software modeling pays off when its expected contextual value exceeds the socio-technical friction required to create and sustain it. Its value is therefore contingent, not universal.

Abstract: This paper introduces a Theory of Developer Momentum that helps us see the activity of developing software in motion and the skilled craft through which progress unfolds. We define Developer Momentum as the developer's experience of progress and forward motion toward the goal of a task. Our theory disentangles the related constructs of flow state, momentum, and motivation, so we can more clearly see the relationships between them and how they interweave to support progress. In order to make progress, developers build up working context in memory, become immersed in a cognitive puzzle, and gain clarity through motion in uncertain terrain. The theory provides researchers with a rich experiential lens to investigate how developers' cognitive experiences are shifting in emerging AI workflows. Building on prior grounded theory work, we conducted in-depth interviews with a theoretically sampled subset of 12 professional developers, and eight feedback interviews to evaluate resonance and investigate how the theory translates to agentic AI workflows. We followed a Constructivist Grounded Theory approach to construct a theory grounded in empirical data. Our theory contributes a novel paradigm for seeing the motion of developing software at multiple scales and resolutions, seeing the living capacities that support this motion, and expanding what becomes available for improvement, skilled judgment, and craft.

</details>

Abstract: Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) hardware has transformed quantum computing into a practical experimental platform while introducing fundamental challenges associated with hardware noise, decoherence, and probabilistic execution. Although quantum software testing (QST) has rapidly emerged as an active research area, it remains unclear to what extent existing testing methodologies explicitly engage with noisy and hardware-constrained execution environments. This paper presents a systematic empirical landscape analysis of noise-awareness in QST research through a deterministic, taxonomy-guided analytical framework applied to 61 papers published between 2018 and 2025 across eight testing paradigms, contrasting how contributions are framed with how they are evaluated. Among the analyzed papers, 47 (77.0%) are noise-oblivious and only 14 (23.0%) explicitly engage with noise-related execution concerns, while only 11 (18.0%) report evaluation on physical hardware or a noisy simulator. Test generation, the largest paradigm, exhibits a noise-awareness rate of only 14.3%, and debugging and repair 18.2%; coverage-based, metamorphic, and differential testing contain no noise-aware contributions. Noise-aware methodologies emerged late and remain outnumbered in all analyzed years. Framing and evaluation are strongly associated (odds ratio 40.5, p < 0.001) yet diverge for individual papers: 5 of the 14 noise-aware papers evaluate only on ideal simulators. Inferential statistical tests are reported in only 16.4% of papers. The classification framework was validated against a blinded, human-coded reference sample, achieving almost perfect inter-rater agreement on testing-technique classification and substantial agreement on noise-awareness. The framework provides a reusable empirical basis for longitudinal analysis of hardware-conscious testing practice.

</details>

Abstract: Commit messages provide records of code changes and help developers understand and maintain software. To reduce the manual effort involved in writing them, commit message generation (CMG) approaches automatically generate commit messages that describe code changes in natural language. Existing CMG approaches generally treat CMG as a standalone task and optimize generated messages primarily for similarity to developer-written references. However, this objective may not properly reflect the practical usefulness of generated commit messages. This limitation matters in current AI-assisted software engineering workflows, where commit messages are used as shared information to serve different software engineering activities. To fill this gap, we propose TraceMsg, which uses issue-commit traceability, a representative software engineering activity, to guide commit message generation. TraceMsg is a collaborative multi-agent framework that coordinates message generation, traceability reasoning, and feedback-guided refinement, allowing generated messages to serve traceability while using reasoning feedback to guide issue-conditioned refinement. We evaluate TraceMsg on six open-source projects. The experimental results show that TraceMsg achieves an average Hit@1 of 0.893 and an MRR of 0.911, improving Hit@1 and MRR by 13.16% and 38.75%, respectively, over the best baseline for each metric.

Abstract: As software agents increasingly generate and modify code, evaluating their ability to review changes and discover defects becomes important. Such evaluation needs executable project environments for investigation and reliable defect references supported by reproducible behavior. Existing code review benchmarks provide limited support for this combination. We present a system that constructs executable regression instances from historical introducing--fixing commit pairs and developer tests. The system extracts candidate tests from fixing-side changes, then uses constrained agents to transplant them and configure version-specific environments while preserving production code and test expectations. Independent replay and evidence-based review verify whether tests that fail on the introducing version and pass on the fixing version support the proposed regression relationship. Accepted instances retain both versions' replay recipes, validated tests, and execution evidence. Repair-supported analysis and human review add root-cause locations, tested repairs, and causal explanations. These instances support bug detection and localization in code review, and enable bug repair and regression-test generation on the same defects. The system constructs 172 validated instances from 522 candidates across 11 Python projects. We evaluate three software agents with ten models and additional reasoning-effort settings on 83 manually selected, enriched instances across the three tasks. Precise root-line localization remains challenging, while baseline combinations achieve repair and regression-test generation success rates of up to 68.7% and 77.1%, respectively. Leading configurations differ by task, and higher task performance does not necessarily yield greater API-cost efficiency. Our approach provides reusable executable evidence for studying agents' defect discovery, repair, and testing capabilities.

These findings establish an empirical baseline for dependency assessability and known-vulnerability exposure in construction-related open-source software. Abstract: Fault localization (FL) aims to identify code locations responsible for software failures and is commonly evaluated by how accurately it ranks known fault locations. Large language model (LLM) agents are changing program repair and are increasingly used to search and reason over repositories. Although recent LLM-based FL methods have improved localization accuracy, their impact on agentic repair remains unclear. To address this gap, we conduct a systematic empirical study of FL in agentic repair. We evaluate six recent FL methods on SWE-bench Verified and Defects4J with three LLMs under a common repair framework and analyze 16,248 repair trajectories. We also examine gold locations and bug-cause descriptions as forms of FL guidance across different repair contexts. The studied FL methods do not consistently improve repair success, and higher localization accuracy does not consistently lead to larger repair gains. Yet FL guides agents' early search and helps them reach relevant code sooner. Accurate locations can reduce repair token use by up to 39.2\%, but current FL methods often consume more tokens than they save during repair. Experiments with gold guidance suggest that FL has greater potential when bug inputs contain fewer location clues. Providing bug causes can further improve repair success beyond locations alone. We also find that ground truth from a single reference patch does not fully capture the locations used by successful repairs. These findings help clarify the value of FL in agentic repair and inform future work on its design and evaluation.

</details>

Abstract: Software engineering (SWE) benchmarks are the primary way to measure the capabilities of coding agents: an agent is placed in a code repository or machine environment, given a request, and judged by hidden tests. These benchmarks almost always start the agent from a clean state that contains no earlier work on the task. In practice, however, agents often continue work that a developer or another agent has already done and reported as complete, with no indication of what remains incorrect or missing. We introduce SWE-Residual, a benchmark for Starting-State Robustness: the ability to maintain performance on a task when the starting state already contains earlier work on that task. SWE-Residual contains 198 Residual States drawn from 111 tasks in DeepSWE and Terminal-Bench, where the earlier work modifies a code repository or a whole machine environment. Each Residual State is the state a predecessor agent left behind after submitting its work as complete, although the hidden tests still fail. The evaluated agent receives only the original request and this state, without the predecessor's trajectory or any sign that the attempt failed; because the request and hidden tests are unchanged, the starting state is the only difference from the source benchmark. Across five frontier agents, success drops by 20.6--53.5 percentage points on DeepSWE and 20.6--34.9 points on Terminal-Bench relative to a Clean Start, and agent rankings on DeepSWE reverse. Failed continuations show a pattern we call acceptance bias: agents treat the inherited work as the definition of what remains to be done instead of checking it against the request. On DeepSWE, in 58.9--88.2% of failed runs, they submit with exactly the same tests failing, although they pass most of these tests from a Clean Start. SWE-Residual thus exposes a capability gap that clean-start evaluation does not capture.

</details>

Abstract: Bounded model checking unrolls a program into a single formula, so a function holding a loop costs the solver a fresh copy of that loop at every call site. Function contracts break that coupling. Verify the body once against its contract, then replace each call by a stub derived from it. CBMC supports this through dynamic frame condition checking, applied at industrial scale, but with no formal account of why the replacement is sound and no characterisation of what the frame check costs.
  We present the function-contract framework for ESBMC. The clause syntax, both phases, and the first assigns enforcement the tool has had are all introduced here. Its technical core is an observational frame rule: snapshot the locations the contract does not permit the function to write, then compare them at exit. The encoding emits a number of verification conditions set by how many globals the program has, not by how many writes the body performs, while dynamic frame condition checking grows with the write count. That is a property of the encoding rather than a timing, so it moves with neither the machine nor the solver. The rule itself is the classical one; what we add is its bounded form inside a BMC encoding, with the pipeline's algebraic skeleton machine-checked in Lean 4 and the limits of that model stated.
  We evaluate on synthetic sweeps, SV-COMP tasks, an ANSSI X.509 parser, the FreeRTOS heap_1 allocator, and the ML-KEM reference implementation whose CBMC contracts we reuse unchanged. At the coefficient count FIPS-203 mandates, neither tool finishes on the monolithic route inside ten minutes, and both finish on the modular one. The framework is merged into ESBMC master. Contracts are what is left when unrolling runs out of room.

</details>

Abstract: Architectural roles describe how responsibilities are distributed within software systems. Entity--Boundary--Control (EBC) separates domain information, external interaction, and workflow coordination, but its proportions across diverse software remain unclear. This study examines file-level classifier-derived EBC proportionality in 1,556,985 Java files from 732 project instances and 78 functional categories. A classifier trained on labels generated by a large language model assigned one representative role per file. The labels were aggregated at project and functional-category levels and analysed using descriptive statistics and density-based clustering. The results show that Entity was less common than Boundary and Control. The dominant cluster covered 48 categories, 472 project instances, and 47.75% of classified files; its mean composition was 13.25% Entity, 42.65% Boundary, and 44.10% Control. The remaining 30 categories form one alternative and 11 smaller specialised patterns. Some selected variations were consistent with category responsibilities, while others received mixed support. Agreement with independent expert labels was limited (31.37% accuracy; macro-F1 0.31; Entity recall 13%). All reported proportions and clusters are therefore patterns in classifier-assigned roles, not verified architectural ground truth. Within this measurement boundary, the study provides a large-scale empirical reference of EBC distributions at the file level, and it documents the limitations of the automated approach.

</details>

Abstract: Coding agents increasingly undertake development tasks in mature enterprise repositories, yet existing benchmarks offer limited insight into whether they fulfill complete Product Requirement Documents (PRDs) and where delivery breaks down. Issue resolution and development from scratch leave incremental PRD fulfillment underexplored, while isolated tests provide incomplete evidence of business and integration requirements. We introduce PACE, a benchmark of 43 enterprise tasks with 750 expert-audited Code Points. It combines requirement reconstruction, criterion construction, and evidence-based evaluation to measure PRD fulfillment and connect delivery gaps to agent behavior. Across 11 model--scaffold configurations, most scores fall between 52.1 and 78.1; GPT-5.6-Sol achieves the highest complete-delivery rate of 14.0\%. Incomplete implementation and missing integration are the principal bottlenecks: producing individual components does not ensure a complete delivery. Evaluation achieves 95.2\% agreement with expert consensus, with lower case-level variation across judging records than repeated executions. Beyond model comparison, the benchmark provides actionable feedback for harness refinement: diagnosis guides targeted improvements, and successive harness releases raise mean performance from 59.4 to 63.0 using the same model and task suite. Code is available at https://github.com/TianqiLiu1/PACE-Bench.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate Verus contracts for Rust programs together with the proofs that establish them. Yet verifier acceptance, the common measure of success, shows only that the implementation satisfies the generated contract, so it leaves open whether the contract captures the intended behavior. Assessing specification generation therefore requires an evaluation methodology, not a single metric. We present VerusEval, a framework that evaluates generated contracts along six dimensions, including Verus-native checks for semantic triviality, validated input-output (I/O) suites, and contract equivalence, and that relates them so that the strength and blind spots of each become measurable. Evaluating 13,716 artifacts from four workflows, five LLMs, and two prompting settings on 762 tasks, we find that acceptance overstates quality: only 42.3\% of the accepted artifacts complete all three semantic checks without a flag, and across workflows and models, higher acceptance does not consistently come with more correct contracts. I/O evaluation rejects 23.6\% of the accepted artifacts, yet only 63.2\% of those that pass it are proved equivalent. Of the accepted artifacts flagged by contract comparison, 84.1\% have a rejected completeness obligation, and the direction of a failure prioritizes inspection of the generated contract or the reference; it does not by itself establish which artifact is defective. Screening and review further identify 36 candidate defects among 61 selected reference contracts, and repairing one of them makes 10 of its 11 failing artifacts equivalent. Based on these findings, we derive four actionable lessons for evaluation, generation, and benchmark maintenance.

</details>
